review-code-for-security-vulnerabilities\ndescription: >-\n  Reviews application source code for security vulnerabilities. First works out\n  what the code actually does, then searches THIS skill library for the\n  specialised skills that match the code's security-relevant behaviour, then\n  applies those skills to audit the code and reports findings with CWE ids and\n  fixes. Use this whenever asked to review, audit, or security-check source\n  code, do a secure code review, find vulnerabilities, or check code against the\n  OWASP Top 10 or CWE. Keywords: code review, secure code review, source code\n  audit, find vulnerabilities, security assessment of code, OWASP, CWE, SQL\n  injection, weak cryptography, hardcoded secrets, insecure deserialization,\n  XSS, SSRF, authentication and session flaws, JWT verification, password\n  storage.\ndomain: cybersecurity\nsubdomain: application-security\ntags:\n  - code-review\n  - security-review\n  - owasp\n  - cwe\n  - sast\n  - dispatcher\nversion: \"1.1\"\nlicense: Apache-2.0\n---\n# Reviewing Code for Security Vulnerabilities\n\n## Overview\n\nThis is a router skill. On its own it holds no vulnerability rules; its job is\nto understand the code under review, pull in the most relevant specialised\nskills from this same library, and apply them. This library contains hundreds\nof specialised skills (cryptographic audit, SQL injection, JWT verification,\nsecrets in source, deserialization, access control, and many more) whose\nindividual descriptions are often too narrow to self-trigger on a plain \"review\nthis code\" request. This skill is the entry point that finds and uses them.\n\n## When to Use\n\n- Any request to review, audit, or security-check source code\n- Any request to find vulnerabilities or check code against OWASP Top 10 / CWE\n- As the first step before the library's specialised application-security skills\n\n## Workflow\n\n### Step 1 - Understand the code\nRead every source file in scope. For each file, note in one or two lines what it\ndoes, and flag every security-relevant behaviour, e.g.:\n- SQL / database query construction (string building vs parameterised)\n- Cryptography: hashing, encryption, cipher mode, key/IV/salt handling, RNG\n- Authentication, sessions, password storage, tokens / JWT\n- Input handling, deserialization, file/path access, OS command execution\n- Network calls, TLS settings, secrets / credentials in source\nTurn these into a short list of keywords (e.g. \"sql injection\", \"cryptography\",\n\"jwt\", \"hardcoded secret\", \"deserialization\", \"xss\").\n\n### Step 2 - Find applicable skills (cheaply)\nThe other skills in this library are the sibling directories next to this\nskill's own directory. Do NOT read them all. Instead:\n1. Find this skill's directory, then its parent (the library root).\n2. Grep skill NAMES first for your Step 1 keywords, e.g. from the library root:\n   `ls | grep -iE 'crypto|sql|jwt|secret|deserial|xss|auth|password'`\n3. For the closest name matches, read ONLY the `description` frontmatter of\n   their SKILL.md (`sed -n '1,15p' <name>/SKILL.md`).\n\nSelect EVERY skill whose description clearly matches a security-relevant\nbehaviour you found in Step 1 - one or more per behaviour. Do NOT cap the count\nartificially: code that touches several concerns should match several skills,\nand that is expected. Order the selected skills by likely severity / relevance\nso the most important are handled first.\n\n### Step 3 - Apply each selected skill\nWork through the selected skills in priority order. Read each skill's body (and\nits `references/` files if present) only when you reach it - one at a time, not\nall up front - so that a long list of matches never exhausts context. Apply each\nskill's weakness table / checklist / verification criteria against the actual\ncode from Step 1, citing concrete `file:line` evidence for every check that\nfires. Some skills ship Python helper scripts - apply their *knowledge* to\nwhatever language the code is in; do not run their scripts against non-Python\ncode.\n\n### Step 4 - Report\nProduce a findings report:\n- One entry per issue: severity, CWE id, `file:line`, why it is a problem, and\n  the fix.\n- A \"Skills applied\" list naming the specialised skills you used.\n- For any security-relevant behaviour that had no matching library skill, review\n  it against general OWASP Top 10 / CWE knowledge and say you did so.\n- If nothing is wrong, say so explicitly and list what you checked.\n\n## Constraints\n\n- Report only. Do not create or modify any files unless explicitly asked.\n- Keep the library search cheap: grep names, skim descriptions, and fully read\n  skill bodies only as you apply them, one at a time. Threat model — employee portal authentication\n\nScope: the sign-in feature (CLI + HTTPS portal) in `com.portal.auth`.\nReviewer stance assumed: a network-resident attacker, an authenticated but malicious\nemployee, a malicious/compromised operator, and a stolen credential dump.\n\nEvery number and identifier below was re-read from the source, not carried over from an\nearlier draft. An earlier revision of this file quoted back-off ceilings that were\nswapped, a session-id length that was off by one character, an\n`includeSubDomains` attribute the code never emitted, and two class names that do not\nexist. A threat model that has drifted from the code is worse than none, because it is\nread as a claim that someone checked.\n\n## 1. Inputs crossing the trust boundary\n\nEvery row below is treated as attacker-controlled. \"Gate\" = the code that must vouch\nfor the value before it is used.\n\n| # | Input | Arrives via | Gate |\n|---|-------|-------------|------|\n| 1 | username | CLI stdin, `POST /login` form field | `InputPolicy.isUsername` — 3–32 chars, `[a-z0-9._-]`, alphanumeric first and last, no `..`; no trimming, ever |\n| 2 | password | `Console.readPassword`, private pipe, form field | `InputPolicy.secret` + `strengthFailure` — 12–128 chars, printable ASCII `0x21..0x7E` only, no leading/trailing space, no control chars, no unpaired surrogates; never trimmed |\n| 3 | one typed line (CLI) | stdin, possibly a pipe | `PortalCli` — 1024-char cap, CR terminates a line rather than being deleted, over-long line aborts the field |\n| 4 | roster file (`employees.dat`) | disk, operator/backup/other process | `EmployeeStore.load`/`parse` — 8 MiB file cap, 256-byte line cap, strict `u=<name> r=<record>`, name re-validated, KDF-iteration floor, canonical base64, duplicate name ⇒ refuse the whole load, 50 000-account cap, file mode + `NOFOLLOW` |\n| 5 | throttle ledger (`throttle.log`) | disk | `Ledger` — 4 MiB / 50 000-line cap, `v2\\|kind:subject\\|windowStart\\|count\\|blockedUntil\\|generation` grammar, key must be `a:`/`s:` + 16 hex, values range-checked, unparsable line ⇒ refuse |\n| 6 | audit log (`audit.log`) | disk | write-only from this process; never parsed for a decision; 200 000-line retention |\n| 7 | config file (`portal.properties`) | disk, `PORTAL_CONFIG` | `PortalConfig.readConfigFile` — 16 KiB cap, mode must have no group/other bits, symlink refused, **unknown key ⇒ refuse to start**; every value type/range/charset checked |\n| 8 | env vars (`PORTAL_DATA_DIR`, `PORTAL_CONFIG`, `PORTAL_KEYSTORE`, `PORTAL_KEYSTORE_PASSWORD_FILE`, `PORTAL_TRUSTED_PROXY_CIDRS`) | process env | `PortalConfig.fromEnvironment` — same gates; paths must be absolute, may not contain a `..` segment, must not descend through a world-writable ancestor |\n| 9 | JVM system properties | command line | same loader; cannot lower a security floor |\n| 10 | session cookie (`PORTAL_SESSION`) | HTTP request | `InputPolicy.isOpaqueToken` (43 base64url chars = 32 bytes) then `SessionManager.find` — **hash → server-side lookup**; absent/unknown/expired ⇒ anonymous |\n| 11 | CSRF cookie + form field | HTTP request | `InputPolicy.isOpaqueToken` (32 chars = 24 bytes) + constant-time compare; both present and equal, single-use (consumed on use), 15-min TTL, 50 000-entry cap |\n| 12 | request body | HTTP | 8 KiB hard cap before parse, `Content-Length` required (no chunked), `application/x-www-form-urlencoded` pinned, known keys only, duplicate keys refused |\n| 13 | `X-Forwarded-For` | HTTP | **ignored unless** the peer is inside `portal.trusted.proxy.cidrs`; then strict manual CIDR parse — no DNS, no `InetAddress.getByName`, all-digit prefix, no leading zero, no `+` |\n| 14 | TLS keystore + its password file | disk, env | must be supplied together, mode ≤ 0600, symlink refused, type pinned to PKCS12, password file ≤ 512 bytes and printable-ASCII-only, no default password |\n| 15 | CLI argv (subcommand, flags) | operator shell | allowlist of subcommands; `PortalCli.safeToken` gates any token taken from argv to `[A-Za-z0-9-_.:]`, ≤ 80 chars, else `unspecified`; **no secret may be passed on argv** |\n| 16 | clock (`Instant.now`) | OS | expiry windows only; skew never extends a lockout |\n\nNon-inputs, for contrast: session ids, CSRF tokens, salts and derived keys are\n**generated** here (`SecureRandom`, `Crypto.randomUrlToken`), never accepted from a caller.\n\n## 2. Attacks the reviewer will try, and the defence\n\n1. **Username enumeration** (timing / message / status-code differences between\n   \"unknown user\" and \"wrong password\"). → One body (`Access denied.`), one status, one\n   header block, and a `Content-Length` identical for every refusal; the KDF runs\n   against a decoy salt for unknown names so the CPU work matches; every refusal path is\n   padded to a single absolute latency floor. The header block has **no per-branch\n   variant** — see defect 7 below.\n2. **Prefix/suffix folding** — `alice\\t`, `alice `, `alice␀`, `ALICE`, `ａｌｉｃｅ`,\n   `ali\\u006ce`, `alice\\r127.0.0.1`. → No trimming anywhere. The username must match the\n   full-string rules, so an invisible or full-width variant is rejected rather than\n   folded onto a real account. `String.trim`/`strip` is banned in this package: it\n   deletes control characters (≤ U+0020, including NUL and tab). CR terminates an input\n   line instead of being swallowed — see defect 2.\n3. **Lockout as a denial-of-service tool** (attacker spends their own 5 guesses to\n   freeze a colleague's account). → Account back-off starts at 30 s and doubles per\n   repeat trip, capped at **900 s (15 min)** precisely because a long account lock is a\n   weapon an attacker can afford. A correct password clears the account's count, block\n   and strike history. The per-source budget (30 failures / 300 s) is spent *before* the\n   account can be locked by a single address, and the source ceiling is long (8 h)\n   because the blocked party is the attacker's own address, so there is no weapon there.\n4. **Counter reset by restart / log rotation** → the ledger is on disk and survives\n   restart (24 h retention window). Keys are SHA-256 refs, so a stolen roster does not\n   reveal who is locked. Pruning is window-based, never \"delete everything\".\n5. **Check-then-act races** → within a process, the decision and the counter mutation\n   happen in one `synchronized` transition that returns the verdict. Across processes,\n   `Ledger` and (since defect 6) `EmployeeStore` each take an exclusive `FileLock` on a\n   sibling lock file and re-read the file while holding it, so a short-lived CLI process\n   cannot write over a sibling's change. Serialising the *writer* is not sufficient for the\n   roster, because the authenticating process is a long-lived *reader* (see defect 8): it\n   re-checks the file's identity before every decision and re-adopts it when it changed.\n6. **Brute force with the server's CPU budget** → PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256, 150 000 iterations\n   (≈ 215 ms/derivation, measured on an idle instance of this box; the cost is linear, so\n   100 000 ≈ 165 ms and 2 000 000 ≈ 2.85 s), floor 100 000, ceiling 2 000 000; a record\n   outside that range is refused at the load boundary, never honoured; the per-source budget\n   bounds aggregate cost.\n7. **Session fixation / prediction / reuse** → ids are 256-bit `SecureRandom`, only the\n   SHA-256 of the id is stored, a session is minted only *after* successful\n   authentication, cookies are `HttpOnly; Secure; SameSite=Strict; Path=/`, idle 20 min +\n   absolute 8 h, 10 000-session cap, logout removes state and expires the cookie.\n8. **CSRF-driven login** (attacker logs the victim into the attacker's account) → login\n   requires a cookie+field token pair the server issued, single-use, 15-min TTL.\n9. **Leak through logs/errors** → the audit line grammar is\n   `ts=… event=… subject=<16hex> src=<16hex> outcome=… detail=…`; `ref()` accepts only a\n   16-hex ref or `-`/`invalid`, and `token()` character-gates, so an attacker-supplied\n   string cannot forge a field. No path can put a password, session id or keystore\n   password into a line (tests assert on captured output). Stack traces go to the log,\n   never the response.\n10. **Leak through files** → roster, ledger, audit log, lock files and keystore are\n    created `0600` inside a `0700` directory; a pre-existing file with group/other bits\n    is refused rather than used; writes go temp→fsync→`ATOMIC_MOVE`.\n11. **Credential in memory** → passwords are `char[]` end to end, wiped in a `finally`;\n    no `String` copy of a password is constructed.\n12. **Supply chain** → zero third-party runtime code (JDK 21 built-ins only). The one\n    network fetch in the build is the JDK tarball, SHA-256-checked against Adoptium's\n    published digest (`tools/get-jdk.sh`).\n13. **Planted code or data** → data dir must be owned by us, not world-writable, with no\n    world-writable ancestor; the launcher fixes the classpath to its own `out/main`,\n    clears `CLASSPATH`, and uses no `.`-discovery (verified: running from a directory\n    containing a planted `com/portal/auth/Main.class` does not load it).\n14. **Oversized input as memory DoS** → every reader is bounded (§1). Unknown or\n    unparsable ⇒ refuse, never \"best effort\".\n15. **Downgrade / cleartext** → HTTPS only; with no keystore configured the portal\n    refuses to start rather than fall back to HTTP.\n16. **Permissive defaults** → every unsatisfiable startup or consistency check ends the\n    process with a generic message. There is no default for an unrecognised value,\n    unknown config key, unknown subcommand or unparsable record.\n\n## 3. Defects found by attacking this build\n\nEach was found while trying to report something, not while writing the code, and each has\na test that fails if it returns. Listed in the order found, with what made it reportable.\n\n| # | Defect | How it was found | How it is now tested |\n|---|--------|------------------|----------------------|\n| 1 | The `..` guard on `PORTAL_DATA_DIR` ran on the **normalised** path — normalising deletes `..`, so the guard could never fire. Startup then created a fresh 0700 directory under `/etc`. | Read the guard, then traced what `Path.normalize()` returns. | `ConfigCliSuite`: exit code 2 + machine token + a before/after diff of the target directory's contents. Mutant M13 restores the old guard and is killed. |\n| 2 | A carriage return was **deleted** from input instead of terminating the line, so `alice\\r127.0.0.1` folded onto the account `alice` and the trailing text became the next field. | Fed raw CR bytes through the real binary. | `PortalCli` CR-terminates (with 1-slot pushback for CRLF); `ConfigCliSuite` drives real CR/CRLF/LF transcripts. Mutant M18 reverts it and is killed. |\n| 3 | `set-password` on a **disabled** account silently re-enabled it. | Asked what a reviewer would say about an operation that un-disable's an account while looking like a password change. | The audit detail distinguishes `updated` from `updated-re-enabled`, the CLI warns, and `isDisabled` exposes the state. Mutant M17 forces the flag false and is killed. |\n| 4 | Integer-valued config/CIDR fields leaned on `parseInt`, which accepts a leading `+`, so `portal.listen.port=+8443` or `10.0.0.0/+8` parsed. | Tried the character each validator *forgot*. | Explicit all-digit grammar checks; `+8`/`+0` fixtures in `SourceRefSuite` and `ConfigCliSuite`. Mutants M14/M15. |\n| 5 | The durable failure counter was **merge-monotonic** (`max(count)`), so a success's `count=0` was immediately restored from the stale on-disk value: the **6th consecutive correct login was denied** while every individual login succeeded. | Reproduced the suite's payload order against one ledger and read the audit `detail=` token. | A per-subject `generation` field in the `v2` ledger format; `AuthFlowSuite` asserts 8 consecutive correct logins all succeed *and* that a hostile burst still locks. Mutant M12 goes monotonic again and is killed. |\n| 6 | `EmployeeStore` serialised roster writes with an **in-process monitor only**, so concurrent CLI processes lost updates: **8 concurrent `add-user` runs all exited 0 and 6 of the 8 accounts were silently discarded.** | Ran 8 real `add-user` processes against one data directory, three times. | Every mutator now takes an exclusive `FileLock` and re-reads the file under it; `ConfigCliSuite` launches 8 writers and asserts all 8 records survive, each writer's own exit code, and that a contested name is won exactly once. Mutants M24/M25. |\n| 7 | `Strict-Transport-Security` was omitted on the 404/405/415/500 replies and sent on the authentication replies, so the **header block itself distinguished the branches** — while its own javadoc claimed every reply carried the same headers. | Booted the portal with a generated keystore and compared a real 404 against a real 401. | `securityHeaders()` takes no flag; `HttpSuite` asserts every response cell has an **identical** header set and each sends HSTS; `CoreSecuritySuite` refuses the return of a `withHsts` parameter. |\n| 8 | The long-lived `portal` process authenticated against the roster copy it read at start-up, while `disable-user` and `set-password` are **separate short-lived processes**. After `disable-user` had written `r=disabled$v1` the running portal still answered `303`; after `set-password`, the **superseded** password still answered `303`. The operator believed a revocation had landed that had not. | Ran the real portal on a real port, drove the CLI against the same data directory, and read the status code back. | `EmployeeStore.refreshIfChanged()` compares the roster's file key + size + mtime and reloads under the monitor; it is the first statement of `Authenticator.decide`, so its one `stat` costs the same on every branch and is a function of the file, never of the presented name. A roster that stops parsing fails **closed** to an empty map (logged once, never rewritten) and recovers without latching. `AuthFlowSuite.rosterPropagation` covers disable→deny, rotate→old password dead / new password live, enrolment without restart, and the fail-closed/no-latch pair. Mutants M26/M27/M28. |\n\nTwo observations that were *not* defects but changed the tests:\n\n- `java.util.Properties` strips leading value whitespace and keeps trailing, so\n  `portal.listen.host = 127.0.0.1 ` yields an invalid literal that is (correctly) refused.\n  Pinned as documented behaviour in `ConfigCliSuite` rather than worked around.\n- A test must own only what its own process does. \"The directory must not exist\" was\n  replaced by \"this run neither created it nor wrote into it\" after a mutation-battery\n  run left a root-owned `/etc/portal-x` behind and made every honest run afterwards fail\n  a check unrelated to the code under test.\n- A **surviving mutant is a test gap, not a code bug**, and the two must be told apart by\n  reading the code rather than by deleting the mutant. M23 (drop `recordFailure` from the\n  unknown-username branch) survived the whole suite. The production behaviour is correct,\n  but nothing in the suite could notice its removal, because every existing source-block\n  assertion sprayed *known* accounts — and a known account's own block was arming the\n  ledger row that made the source look blocked. `beginAttempt` only *counts*; the timed\n  window is armed exclusively by `recordFailure`, so an unknown-name spray would have kept\n  a throttled attacker on a 300 s rolling window instead of the 8 h ceiling. The suite now\n  sprays only names that do not exist and asserts the *source* is blocked and its armed\n  window reaches the ceiling.\n- Two helpers turned a **lossy charset into a security decision**, on inputs that were\n  already validated, so nothing was broken: `idRef(String)` hashed\n  `getBytes(US_ASCII)`, which replaces any non-ASCII character with `'?'` — two different\n  names would have shared one throttle bucket — and `equalConstantTime` did the same to the\n  two CSRF operands before comparing them. Both call sites validate first\n  (`InputPolicy.isUsername` / `isCsrfToken`), so the fold was unreachable; it is still what\n  a reviewer would report, because the transform is one edit away from the decision. Both\n  now refuse non-ASCII instead of encoding it, and `CoreSecuritySuite` pins the refusal in\n  both directions (\"`café` vs `café`\" must not compare equal, and `'?'` is not a character\n  any account name may contain). Mutants M29/M30 restore each fold and are killed. The same\n  pass corrected `idRef`'s javadoc, which described the 16-hex-character truncation as\n  \"128 bits\" when it is 64 — the README had the number right and the comment did not.\n\n## 4. Deliberate deviations (documented residual risk)\n\n- **Externally supplied identifier**: a session id is *accepted* from the cookie, but\n  only as a lookup key into server-side state; it grants nothing by itself. Residual\n  risk: a stolen cookie on a TLS-terminated connection is usable until expiry — bounded\n  by 20 min idle / 8 h absolute, `SameSite=Strict`, HTTPS-only.\n- **`X-Forwarded-For`** is ignored by default. Enabling it via\n  `portal.trusted.proxy.cidrs` trusts those peers to have set it; wrong config =\n  per-source throttling becomes spoofable. Per-*account* throttling is unaffected.\n- **Per-source limit and a shared NAT**: a large office behind one address shares the\n  per-source budget. The CLI's own source label is the constant `local`, so all CLI\n  logins on one host share one budget by construction.\n- **Password strength rules are enforced at enrolment only.** Applying them at login\n  would create a second, distinguishable failure reason.\n- **HSTS is emitted without `includeSubDomains` or `preload`**: this host is not\n  necessarily a domain root, and widening the policy to subdomains the operator does not\n  control is a worse failure than a narrower one.\n- **The roster lock file is advisory.** `FileLock` is process-local: it serialises the\n  CLI and the portal on one host, not two hosts sharing a network filesystem. Two\n  writers over NFS can still lose an update. Residual risk accepted because the roster\n  is documented as host-local state (rule 5's owner-private directory), and a writer who\n  can reach the file can delete it anyway.\n- **The ledger merge is generation-based, not record-based.** Concurrent writers on the\n  same subject keep the higher generation's count and the larger `blockedUntil`; two\n  processes that each recorded one failure in the same window may collapse to one count.\n  That direction under-reports a *source* by at most one increment per writer and never\n  clears a block, so it cannot widen the budget. Same residual-risk boundary as above.\n- **PBKDF2 over Argon2id**: the JDK ships no memory-hard KDF, and rule 8 says prefer the\n  platform over a hand-rolled or third-party primitive. 150 000 PBKDF2-SHA256 rounds is\n  the platform's best provable option; the record format already carries a version field\n  so a memory-hard implementation can be dropped in behind it.\n- **`com.sun.net.httpserver`** is a JDK-bundled HTTP engine, not a hardened server.\n  Mitigated by the request-shape limits in §1 and by refusing anything unexpected;\n  it registers a single context (`/`) whose dispatcher serves exactly two routes,\n  `/login` and `/logout` — every other path is a 404 with the same header block and body\n  as every other refusal.\n"}]}},{"type":"assistant","uuid":"a5c27c5b-56cc-4dbb-9df4-83b490c44ef5","session_id":"43a3d1fe-7aa6-43e7-b332-fdd94a07da51","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"id":"a5c27c5b-56cc-4dbb-9df4-83b490c44ef5","type":"message","role":"assistant","model":"vllm-general","content":[{"type":"thinking","thinking":"This is a \"hardened\" codebase with a documented threat model claiming everything is fine. Everything else in this\n * program is plumbing around it.\n *\n * <h2>The one-digest invariant</h2>\n * Every outcome of {@link #authenticate} executes <b>exactly one</b> PBKDF2 derivation of\n * the presented password at the configured cost, and no more:\n * <ul>\n *   <li>malformed username / malformed password -&gt; one decoy derivation</li>\n *   <li>budget spent -&gt; one decoy derivation (the roster is never read here)</li>\n *   <li>unknown username -&gt; one decoy derivation</li>\n *   <li>known username -&gt; one real verification</li>\n * </ul>\n * CPU work per request is therefore independent of the outcome, and every refusal is\n * additionally padded to a single shared latency floor. \"Account does not exist\",\n * \"wrong password\", \"bad input\" and \"you are rate-limited\" are not distinguishable by\n * timing, and are not distinguishable at all in the response the caller receives.\n *\n * <h2>What a caller may learn</h2>\n * Exactly one bit: success (with a freshly minted session id) or denial.\n * {@link Result#reasonForInternalUseOnly()} exists so the audit log and the test suite can\n * tell branches apart; it is never rendered into a response body, status code, header,\n * cookie, or byte count that a client can observe.\n *\n * <h2>Username folding</h2>\n * {@code ALICE} and {@code alice} are the same employee. The fold is a 26-letter ASCII\n * table applied <em>before</em> validation -- not {@code toLowerCase()} (locale hazard: the\n * Turkish dotted/dotless i) and not Unicode case folding or NFKC (identity hazard:\n * {@code Kelvin} folds onto {@code Kelin}, and a full-width character folds onto a\n * different name). Because the accepted alphabet is {@code [a-z0-9._-]}, this fold is\n * injective over distinct accepted identities: no two real accounts can ever be folded\n * together, so the convenience costs nothing in identity strength.\n */\npublic final class Authenticator {\n\n    /**\n     * Refused-input decoy reference. Not hex, so it can never collide with a real idRef.\n     * Package-visible because {@link AuditLog} recognises it as one of the two non-digest\n     * tokens the subject field accepts.\n     */\n    static final String INVALID_REF = \"invalid\";\n\n    public enum Outcome {\n        SUCCESS, DENIED\n    }\n\n    /** Machine-readable internal reason. Never surfaced to a client. */\n    public enum Reason {\n        CREDENTIALS_OK, BAD_CREDENTIALS, INPUT_INVALID, THROTTLED, KDF_UNAVAILABLE\n    }\n\n    public static final class Result {\n        private final Outcome outcome;\n        private final Reason reason;\n        private final String sessionId;\n        private final String username;\n\n        Result(Outcome outcome, Reason reason, String sessionId, String username) {\n            this.outcome = outcome;\n            this.reason = reason;\n            this.sessionId = sessionId;\n            this.username = username;\n        }\n\n        public boolean isSuccess() {\n            return outcome == Outcome.SUCCESS;\n        }\n\n        public Outcome outcome() {\n            return outcome;\n        }\n\n        /** Non-null only on success. */\n        public String sessionId() {\n            return sessionId;\n        }\n\n        /** Non-null only on success. */\n        public String username() {\n            return username;\n        }\n\n        public String reasonForInternalUseOnly() {\n            return reason.name();\n        }\n    }\n\n    private final EmployeeStore store;\n    private final PasswordHasher hasher;\n    private final ThrottleManager throttle;\n    private final SessionManager sessions;\n    private final AuditLog audit;\n\n    public Authenticator(EmployeeStore store, PasswordHasher hasher, ThrottleManager throttle,\n                         SessionManager sessions, AuditLog audit) {\n        this.store = store;\n        this.hasher = hasher;\n        this.throttle = throttle;\n        this.sessions = sessions;\n        this.audit = audit;\n    }\n\n    /** CLI entry point: the password arrives as the {@code char[]} Console gave us. */\n    public Result authenticate(char[] username, char[] password, String sourceLabel) {\n        char[] folded = username == null ? new char[0] : Authenticator.foldAsciiCase(username);\n        String presented;\n        try {\n            presented = new String(folded);\n        } finally {\n            Crypto.wipe(username);\n            Crypto.wipe(folded);\n        }\n        return authenticate(presented, password, sourceLabel);\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * @param presentedUsername attacker-controlled; folded and validated below\n     * @param password          attacker-controlled {@code char[]}; <b>cleared by this\n     *                          method</b> on every path, including success\n     * @param sourceLabel       already-reduced source reference (see {@link SourceRef})\n     */\n    public Result authenticate(String presentedUsername, char[] password, String sourceLabel) {\n        long startedAt = System.nanoTime();\n        try {\n            return decide(presentedUsername, password, sourceLabel, startedAt);\n        } finally {\n            // Single exit point for clearing, so no branch can forget it.\n            Crypto.wipe(password);\n        }\n    }\n\n    private Result decide(String presentedUsername, char[] password, String sourceLabel,\n                          long startedAt) {\n        // Adopt another process's roster change before deciding anything. This is the first\n        // statement of the decision so that the cost is the same on every path -- including\n        // the two malformed-input paths and the throttled path, which never look at a record\n        // and must not be distinguishable by a cost the other paths do not pay. The check is\n        // a stat of the roster file, so it is a function of the file, not of the presented\n        // name, and can therefore never answer \"does this account exist?\".\n        store.refreshIfChanged();\n\n        String sourceHex = SourceRef.normalizeForLedger(sourceLabel);\n        String username = presentedUsername == null ? \"\" : foldAsciiCase(presentedUsername);\n\n        // Each branch below performs exactly one key derivation before calling deny();\n        // that is the invariant stated in the class note, and it is why the derivations\n        // are spelled out at every call site instead of being hidden inside deny().\n        if (!InputPolicy.isUsername(username)) {\n            hasher.burnOneDigest(password, INVALID_REF);\n            throttle.consumeSourceBudget(sourceHex);\n            deny(password, INVALID_REF, sourceHex, Reason.INPUT_INVALID, startedAt);\n            return denial(Reason.INPUT_INVALID);\n        }\n\n        String accountRef = Crypto.idRef(username);\n\n        if (!InputPolicy.isWellFormedSecret(password)) {\n            // Counted against the source budget and padded like every other refusal, so\n            // a malformed password is not a cheap probe of the boundary.\n            hasher.burnOneDigest(password, accountRef);\n            throttle.consumeSourceBudget(sourceHex);\n            deny(password, accountRef, sourceHex, Reason.INPUT_INVALID, startedAt);\n            return denial(Reason.INPUT_INVALID);\n        }\n\n        // The slot is reserved (counter incremented) before the password is looked at, so\n        // a concurrent burst cannot all observe the same headroom and then all verify.\n        ThrottleManager.Kind reserved = throttle.beginAttempt(accountRef, sourceHex);\n        if (reserved == ThrottleManager.Kind.THROTTLED) {\n            // The roster is deliberately not consulted here: reading it would make the\n            // throttle response differ in timing between an existing and an unknown name.\n            // The secret is well-formed at this point, so the real decoy path is safe.\n            hasher.verifyAgainstDecoy(password, accountRef);\n            deny(password, accountRef, sourceHex, Reason.THROTTLED, startedAt);\n            return denial(Reason.THROTTLED);\n        }\n\n        Optional<String> record = store.recordFor(username);\n        if (record.isEmpty()) {\n            hasher.verifyAgainstDecoy(password, accountRef);\n            // Counted exactly like a wrong password, so neither the counter behaviour nor\n            // the cost distinguishes an unknown name from a known one.\n            throttle.recordFailure(accountRef, sourceHex);\n            deny(password, accountRef, sourceHex, Reason.BAD_CREDENTIALS, startedAt);\n            return denial(Reason.BAD_CREDENTIALS);\n        }\n\n        boolean matches;\n        try {\n            matches = hasher.verify(password, record.get(), accountRef);\n        } catch (RuntimeException kdfUnavailable) {\n            // A KDF that cannot run must not read as \"wrong password\" (that would let an\n            // outage lock every account out) nor as \"success\". Fail closed and say so in\n            // the log; the client sees the same generic denial as everybody else.\n            audit.systemFailure(\"login\", accountRef, sourceHex, kdfUnavailable);\n            try {\n                hasher.burnOneDigest(password, accountRef);\n            } catch (RuntimeException kdfStillBroken) {\n                // Nothing left to equalise with; the denial stands either way.\n            }\n            deny(password, accountRef, sourceHex, Reason.KDF_UNAVAILABLE, startedAt);\n            return denial(Reason.KDF_UNAVAILABLE);\n        }\n\n        if (!matches) {\n            throttle.recordFailure(accountRef, sourceHex);\n            deny(password, accountRef, sourceHex, Reason.BAD_CREDENTIALS, startedAt);\n            return denial(Reason.BAD_CREDENTIALS);\n        }\n\n        // Verified against server-side state. Upgrade the record when the stored KDF cost\n        // is below policy -- reachable only by someone who already knows the password, so\n        // it cannot be used to make the store more expensive for others.\n        if (hasher.needsRehash(record.get())) {\n            try {\n                store.rotateSecret(username, password);\n                audit.event(\"login\", accountRef, sourceHex, \"ok\", \"granted-rotated\");\n            } catch (RuntimeException rotationFailed) {\n                // Authentication succeeded; a failed write is an operational problem and\n                // must not retroactively change the outcome.\n                audit.systemFailure(\"rotate\", accountRef, sourceHex, rotationFailed);\n            }\n        } else {\n            audit.event(\"login\", accountRef, sourceHex, \"ok\", \"granted\");\n        }\n\n        throttle.recordSuccess(accountRef, sourceHex);\n        String session = sessions.issue(username);\n        // Success is padded on the same floor as the refusals, so an observer measuring\n        // latency cannot tell a granted login from a denied one.\n        throttle.padToFloor(startedAt);\n        throttle.observeLatency(System.nanoTime() - startedAt);\n        return new Result(Outcome.SUCCESS, Reason.CREDENTIALS_OK, session, username);\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Record the refusal, then pad. Note the order: the audit write is charged\n     * <em>before</em> the padding so the padding covers it, and the padding never decides\n     * anything -- it only equalises.\n     */\n    private void deny(char[] password, String subjectRef, String sourceHex, Reason reason,\n                      long startedAt) {\n        audit.event(\"login\", subjectRef, sourceHex, \"denied\", auditDetail(reason));\n        throttle.padToFloor(startedAt);\n        throttle.observeLatency(System.nanoTime() - startedAt);\n    }\n\n    private static String auditDetail(Reason reason) {\n        return switch (reason) {\n            case BAD_CREDENTIALS -> \"bad-credentials\";\n            case INPUT_INVALID -> \"input-invalid\";\n            case THROTTLED -> \"throttled\";\n            case KDF_UNAVAILABLE -> \"kdf-unavailable\";\n            case CREDENTIALS_OK -> \"granted\";\n        };\n    }\n\n    private static Result denial(Reason reason) {\n        return new Result(Outcome.DENIED, reason, null, null);\n    }\n\n    /** Locale-free ASCII A-Z -&gt; a-z. Touches nothing else. See the class note. */\n    static String foldAsciiCase(String in) {\n        return new String(foldAsciiCase(in.toCharArray()));\n    }\n\n    static char[] foldAsciiCase(char[] in) {\n        char[] out = in.clone();\n        for (int i = 0; i < out.length; i++) {\n            if (out[i] >= 'A' && out[i] <= 'Z') {\n                out[i] = (char) (out[i] + 32);\n            }\n        }\n        return out;\n    }\n}\n"}]}},{"type":"user","uuid":"20e133e6-8f1f-4152-93ed-c0bc677730c5","session_id":"43a3d1fe-7aa6-43e7-b332-fdd94a07da51","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"tool_result","tool_use_id":"call_4c4a5299a0224d459760f650","is_error":false,"content":"package com.portal.auth;\n\n/**\n * The single trust boundary for attacker-supplied text.\n *\n * <p>Rules baked in here (see docs/THREAT_MODEL.md section 1):\n * <ul>\n *   <li>No value is trimmed. {@code String.trim()}/{@code strip()} delete characters\n *       {@code <= U+0020} including NUL and TAB, so a trim would silently fold\n *       {@code \"alice\\t\"} onto the real account {@code \"alice\"}. Rejection is the only\n *       acceptable response to an invisible character.</li>\n *   <li>Usernames are printable-ASCII-only from a fixed character class, so case\n *       folding, Unicode normalisation (NFKC) and confusable/similar-codepoint\n *       folding cannot produce a different identity from the same bytes. The accepted\n *       set is already normalised: every accepted username is NFKC-invariant.</li>\n *   <li>Passwords are printable-ASCII-only for the same reason, and because\n *       {@link javax.crypto.spec.PBEKeySpec} is not injective over {@code char[]}\n *       (it drops trailing NULs and maps unpaired surrogates to {@code '?'}), which\n *       would otherwise make two different passwords verify identically.</li>\n *   <li>Every rejection carries a machine-readable code and never the rejected value,\n *       so a rejection cannot leak into a log or a response.</li>\n * </ul>\n */\npublic final class InputPolicy {\n\n    /** Hard ceilings. Anything larger is refused before it is copied or hashed. */\n    public static final int USERNAME_MIN = 3;\n    public static final int USERNAME_MAX = 32;\n    public static final int PASSWORD_MIN = 12;\n    public static final int PASSWORD_MAX = 128;\n    /** A line read from a file/pipe is refused above this, before any parsing. */\n    public static final int LINE_MAX = 1024;\n    /** Opaque token shapes: 32 random bytes => 43 base64url chars, 24 => 32 chars. */\n    public static final int SESSION_TOKEN_BYTES = 32;\n    public static final int CSRF_TOKEN_BYTES = 24;\n\n    private InputPolicy() {\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Accepted usernames: {@code [a-z0-9._-]{3,32}} whose first and last characters are\n     * alphanumeric. Lower-case only on purpose: an upper-case-accepting scheme must\n     * case-fold to look the account up, and case-folding is exactly the step where\n     * identity-folding bugs (and Turkish-i style surprises) live.\n     */\n    public static boolean isUsername(String candidate) {\n        if (candidate == null) {\n            return false;\n        }\n        return isUsername(candidate.toCharArray());\n    }\n\n    public static boolean isUsername(char[] c) {\n        if (c == null || c.length < USERNAME_MIN || c.length > USERNAME_MAX) {\n            return false;\n        }\n        if (!isAlnum(c[0]) || !isAlnum(c[c.length - 1])) {\n            return false;\n        }\n        for (char ch : c) {\n            if (!isAlnum(ch) && ch != '.' && ch != '_' && ch != '-') {\n                return false;\n            }\n        }\n        // Refuse the dot-leading/trailing and \"..\" shapes that confuse path and\n        // normalisation handling downstream, even though they cannot be a valid name.\n        String s = new String(c);\n        return !s.contains(\"..\");\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Structural validation of a password candidate: printable ASCII only, correct\n     * length, no leading/trailing space (a copy-paste artefact that is impossible to\n     * see), no NUL/control characters, no surrogates.\n     */\n    public static boolean isWellFormedSecret(char[] secret) {\n        if (secret == null || secret.length < PASSWORD_MIN || secret.length > PASSWORD_MAX) {\n            return false;\n        }\n        if (secret[0] == ' ' || secret[secret.length - 1] == ' ') {\n            return false;\n        }\n        for (char c : secret) {\n            if (c < 0x21 || c > 0x7E) {\n                return false;\n            }\n        }\n        return true;\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Enrolment-time strength rules. Deliberately NOT applied at login time: a login\n     * that answers \"your password is too weak\" is a second distinguishable failure\n     * reason and hands an attacker an enumeration channel.\n     */\n    public static String strengthFailure(char[] secret, char[] username) {\n        if (!isWellFormedSecret(secret)) {\n            return \"Password must be \" + PASSWORD_MIN + \"-\" + PASSWORD_MAX\n                    + \" printable ASCII characters with no leading or trailing space.\";\n        }\n        int lower = 0;\n        int upper = 0;\n        int digit = 0;\n        int other = 0;\n        for (char c : secret) {\n            if (c >= 'a' && c <= 'z') {\n                lower++;\n            } else if (c >= 'A' && c <= 'Z') {\n                upper++;\n            } else if (c >= '0' && c <= '9') {\n                digit++;\n            } else {\n                other++;\n            }\n        }\n        if (lower + upper + digit + other != secret.length) {\n            return \"Password contains a character that is not permitted.\";\n        }\n        if (lower == 0 || upper == 0 || digit == 0 || other == 0) {\n            return \"Password must contain a lower-case letter, an upper-case letter, \"\n                    + \"a digit and a non-alphanumeric character.\";\n        }\n        if (containsIgnoreCase(secret, username)) {\n            return \"Password must not contain the username.\";\n        }\n        String common = commonShapeFailure(secret);\n        if (common != null) {\n            return common;\n        }\n        return null;\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Rejects shapes that pass the class rules but are on every breach list.\n     *\n     * <p>Two things worth stating explicitly, because the first drafts of both rules were\n     * unreachable and therefore worth nothing:\n     * <ul>\n     *   <li>a \"no single repeated character\" rule that only fires when <em>every</em>\n     *       character is the same can never fire at all -- such a password always fails the\n     *       four-class rule above first. The usable rule is a share bound.</li>\n     *   <li>an ascending-run rule alone leaves {@code dcba9876} (a descending keyboard\n     *       walk) perfectly acceptable. Both directions are checked.</li>\n     * </ul>\n     * Each rule below is exercised by a fixture that passes every <em>other</em> rule, so a\n     * regression that makes one unreachable shows up as a failure rather than as green.\n     */\n    private static String commonShapeFailure(char[] s) {\n        int[] count = new int[128];\n        for (char c : s) {\n            count[c]++;\n        }\n        int most = 0;\n        for (int n : count) {\n            most = Math.max(most, n);\n        }\n        if (most * 2 > s.length) {\n            return \"Password must not be built mostly from one character.\";\n        }\n        int run = 1;\n        for (int i = 1; i < s.length; i++) {\n            int delta = s[i] - s[i - 1];\n            if (delta == 1 || delta == -1) {\n                run++;\n                if (run >= 5) {\n                    return \"Password must not contain a long ascending or descending sequence.\";\n                }\n            } else {\n                run = 1;\n            }\n        }\n        return null;\n    }\n\n    private static boolean containsIgnoreCase(char[] haystack, char[] needle) {\n        if (needle == null || needle.length == 0 || needle.length > haystack.length) {\n            return false;\n        }\n        char[] n = new char[needle.length];\n        for (int i = 0; i < needle.length; i++) {\n            n[i] = lowerAscii(needle[i]);\n        }\n        try {\n            outer:\n            for (int i = 0; i + n.length <= haystack.length; i++) {\n                for (int j = 0; j < n.length; j++) {\n                    if (lowerAscii(haystack[i + j]) != n[j]) {\n                        continue outer;\n                    }\n                }\n                return true;\n            }\n            return false;\n        } finally {\n            Crypto.wipe(n);\n        }\n    }\n\n    private static char lowerAscii(char c) {\n        return (c >= 'A' && c <= 'Z') ? (char) (c + 32) : c;\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Validates a session id or CSRF token that arrived from a client. javax.crypto.spec.PBEKeySpec;\nimport javax.crypto.spec.SecretKeySpec;\n\n/**\n * PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256 password records, using only the JDK's own key derivation.\n *\n * <p>Record text (this is what is written to the roster file, never a password):\n * <pre>pbkdf2$sha256$&lt;iterations&gt;$&lt;base64 salt&gt;$&lt;base64 derived key&gt;</pre>\n *\n * <p>Why PBKDF2 and not Argon2/bcrypt: the JDK ships The\n * iteration count is stored <em>per record</em> and a floor is enforced, so records can\n * be upgraded (and this class replaced by a memory-hard implementation) without a\n * format break -- see {@link #needsRehash(String)}.\n */\npublic final class PasswordHasher {\n\n    public static final String ALG = \"pbkdf2\";\n    public static final String HASH_ALG = \"sha256\";\n    /**\n     * Administrative \"account switched off\" marker. A recognised record shape that can\n     * never verify (it is not a KDF record, so {@link #verify} runs the decoy KDF and\n     * returns false) yet is accepted by the strict roster loader, so disabling one\n     * account cannot take the whole roster down.\n     */\n    public static final String DISABLED_RECORD = \"disabled$v1\";\n    private static final String JCE_KDF = \"PBKDF2WithHmacSHA256\";\n\n    public static final int SALT_BYTES = 16;\n    public static final int KEY_BYTES = 32;\n    /** Records below this are refused outright; they are never honoured. */\n    public static final int MIN_ITERATIONS = 100_000;\n    public static final int MAX_ITERATIONS = 2_000_000;\n    public static final int DEFAULT_ITERATIONS = 150_000;\n\n    private final int configuredIterations;\n\n    public PasswordHasher() {\n        this(DEFAULT_ITERATIONS);\n    }\n\n    public PasswordHasher(int configuredIterations) {\n        if (configuredIterations < MIN_ITERATIONS || configuredIterations > MAX_ITERATIONS) {\n            throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"iterations out of range\");\n        }\n        this.configuredIterations = configuredIterations;\n    }\n\n    public int configuredIterations() {\n        return configuredIterations;\n    }\n\n    /** Derive a fresh record. record.split(\"\\\\$\", -1);\n        if (parts.length != 5) {\n            return null;\n        }\n        int iterations;\n        try {\n            iterations = Integer.parseInt(parts[2]);\n        } catch (NumberFormatException notInt) {\n            return null;\n        }\n        if (iterations < MIN_ITERATIONS || iterations > MAX_ITERATIONS) {\n            return null;\n        }\n badEncoding) {\n            return null;\n        }\n        if (salt.length != SALT_BYTES || key.length != KEY_BYTES) {\n            return null;\n        }\n        return new ParsedRecord(parts[0], parts[1], iterations, salt, key);\n    }\n\n    private static final class ParsedRecord {\n        final String alg;\n        final String hashAlg;\n        final int iterations;\n        final byte[] Everything here is a thin, checked wrapper over a JDK primitive:\n * SHA-256, {@link MessageDigest#isEqual} (constant time) and {@link SecureRandom}.\n * No hand-rolled primitives, no third-party code.\n */\npublic final class Crypto {\n\n    private Crypto() {\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * The single entropy source for every token, salt and key in this program.\n     * The default provider's StrongShadowed/NativePRNG instance is seeded from the OS.\n     */\n    private static final SecureRandom RANDOM = new SecureRandom();\n\n    private static final Base64.Encoder B64URL = Base64.getUrlEncoder().withoutPadding();\n    private static final Base64.Decoder B64URL_DEC = Base64.getUrlDecoder();\n    private static final Base64.Decoder B64STD_DEC = Base64.getDecoder();\n    private static final Base64.Encoder B64STD = Base64.getEncoder();\n\n    public static byte[] randomBytes(int count) {\n        if (count < 16 || count > 4096) {\n            throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"randomBytes count out of range\");\n        }\n        byte[] out = new byte[count];\n        RANDOM.nextBytes(out);\n        return out;\n    }\n\n    /** URL-safe, unpadded base64 of {@code byteCount} random bytes (never caller-supplied). */\n    public static String randomUrlToken(int byteCount) {\n        return B64URL.encodeToString(randomBytes(byteCount));\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Short random suffix for temp/probe filenames. Kept separate from\n     * {@link #randomUrlToken} on purpose: the 16-byte floor of {@link #randomBytes} is a\n     * guard against accidentally minting a weak *security* token, and this is not one --\n     * it only has to defeat a same-instant collision by another process.\n     */\n    public static String randomNameSuffix(int byteCount) {\n        if (byteCount < 6 || byteCount > 15) {\n            throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"name-suffix size out of range\");\n        }\n        byte[] out = new byte[byteCount];\n        RANDOM.nextBytes(out);\n        try {\n            return B64URL.encodeToString(out);\n        } finally {\n            Crypto.wipe(out);\n        }\n    }\n\n    public static byte[] sha256(byte[] in) {\n        return digest(\"SHA-256\").digest(in);\n    }\n\n    public static byte[] sha256(char[] ascii) {\n        return digest(\"SHA-256\").digest(asciiBytes(ascii));\n    }\n\n    public static String sha256Hex(byte[] in) {\n        return toHex(sha256(in));\n    }\n\n    public static String sha256Hex(String in) {\n        return sha256Hex(in.getBytes(StandardCharsets.UTF_8));\n    }\n\n    /** Constant-time comparison; the platform implementation, not ours. */\n    public static boolean equal(byte[] a, byte[] b) {\n        return MessageDigest.isEqual(a, b);\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Length-safe, never-throwing comparison of two {@code char[]} secrets, used for the\n     * \"repeat your new password\" confirmation. Not a security decision (both values came\n     * from the same terminal), but written so it cannot leak a difference through an\n     * exception or an early exit either.\n     */\n    public static boolean equalChars(char[] a, char[] b) {\n        if (a == null || b == null) {\n            return a == b;\n        }\n        int diff = a.length ^ b.length;\n        int n = Math.min(a.length, b.length);\n        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {\n            diff |= a[i] ^ b[i];\n        }\n        return diff == 0;\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Constant-time comparison of two ASCII tokens, on the platform's {@code MessageDigest}\n     * comparison rather than a hand-written loop. Anything non-ASCII is reported as unequal\n     * without being encoded: a charset that cannot represent a character replaces it with\n     * {@code '?'}, which is the one way this comparison could report two different values as\n     * equal. The single production caller validates both operands first\n     * ({@code InputPolicy.isCsrfToken}), so the guard never fires on a live request -- it\n     * exists so that a later caller cannot inherit that failure mode by accident.\n     */\n    public static boolean equalConstantTime(String a, String b) {\n        if (a == null || b == null) {\n            return a == b;\n        }\n        if (!isAscii(a) || !isAscii(b)) {\n            return false;\n        }\n        byte[] x = a.getBytes(StandardCharsets.US_ASCII);\n        byte[] y = b.getBytes(StandardCharsets.US_ASCII);\n        try {\n            return MessageDigest.isEqual(x, y);\n        } finally {\n            wipe(x);\n            wipe(y);\n        }\n    }\n\n    private static boolean isAscii(String s) {\n        for (int i = 0; i < s.length(); i++) {\n            if (s.charAt(i) > 0x7F) {\n                return false;\n            }\n        }\n        return true;\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Strict base64 decode: rejects the empty field, an over-long field, unknown alphabet\n     * characters, stray padding and trailing bits. The empty case matters because\n     * {@code \"pbkdf2$sha256$100000$$<key>\"} has a structurally present but valueless salt\n     * field; decoding it to a zero-length array and letting a length check downstream do\n     * the work would spread the decision across two places.\n     */\n    public static byte[] base64Strict(String s) {\n        if (s == null || s.isEmpty() || s.length() > 512) {\n            throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"base64:length\");\n        }\n        byte[] raw;\n        try {\n            raw = B64STD_DEC.decode(s);\n        } catch (IllegalArgumentException bad) {\n            throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"base64:illegal\");\n        }\n        // Canonical form check: non-canonical encodings decode \"fine\" but are malleable.\n        if (!B64STD.encodeToString(raw).equals(s)) {\n            throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"base64:non-canonical\");\n        }\n        return raw;\n    }\n\n    public static String base64(byte[] in) {\n        return B64STD.encodeToString(in);\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Strict url-safe base64 decode with the same canonical-form requirement, plus an\n     * exact expected byte length. Used for session ids and CSRF tokens, i.e. attacker-\n     * supplied strings that are about to be compared against server-side state.\n     */\n    public static byte[] base64UrlStrictExpectedLen(String s, int expectedBytes) {\n        if (s == null || s.isEmpty() || s.length() > 256) {\n            throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"b64url:length\");\n        }\n        byte[] raw;\n        try {\n            raw = B64URL_DEC.decode(s);\n        } catch (IllegalArgumentException bad) {\n            throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"b64url:illegal\");\n        }\n        if (raw.length != expectedBytes) {\n            throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"b64url:wrong-length\");\n        }\n        if (!B64URL.encodeToString(raw).equals(s)) {\n            throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"b64url:non-canonical\");\n        }\n        return raw;\n    }\n\n    /** Bytes of an already-validated ASCII char array. Refuses anything non-ASCII. */\n    public static byte[] asciiBytes(char[] ascii) {\n        byte[] out = new byte[ascii.length];\n        for (int i = 0; i < ascii.length; i++) {\n            char c = ascii[i];\n            if (c > 0x7F) {\n                Arrays.fill(out, (byte) 0);\n                throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"ascii:non-ascii\");\n            }\n            out[i] = (byte) c;\n        }\n        return out;\n    }\n\n    public static String toHex(byte[] in) {\n        StringBuilder sb = new StringBuilder(in.length * 2);\n        for (byte b : in) {\n            sb.append(Character.forDigit((b >> 4) & 0xF, 16));\n            sb.append(Character.forDigit(b & 0xF, 16));\n        }\n        return sb.toString();\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Short, non-reversible reference to an identity or address: the first 16 hex characters\n     * of the digest, i.e. 64 bits. Truncated so the audit line stays short and the value\n     * cannot be offline-brute-forced back to a name, yet wide enough to double as a\n     * throttle-bucket key -- a collision there would mean two accounts sharing one failure\n     * counter, which is a security outcome. The subject population for a bucket is the set of\n     * names and source addresses seen inside one retention window, so even a six-figure\n     * directory leaves the collision chance near {@code n^2/2^65} (~3e-8 at n = 1e6), far\n     * below anything operationally relevant. The width is pinned on both sides of the ledger\n     * rather than inferred: {@code Ledger.key} refuses a subject that is not exactly\n     * {@code Ledger.SUBJECT_HEX} wide and {@code Ledger.validKey} refuses it again on load.\n     */\n    public static String idRef(char[] ascii) {\n        return sha256Hex(asciiBytes(ascii)).substring(0, 16);\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Same reference as {@link #idRef(char[])} and deliberately routed through it: a lossy\n     * charset conversion here would fold two different names onto one throttle bucket, so\n     * non-ASCII is refused the same way the {@code char[]} overload refuses it instead of\n     * being mapped to {@code '?'}.\n     */\n    public static String idRef(String ascii) {\n        return idRef(ascii.toCharArray());\n    }\n\n    public static void wipe(char[] a) {\n        if (a != null) {\n            Arrays.fill(a, '\\0');\n        }\n    }\n\n    public static void wipe(byte[] a) {\n        if (a != null) {\n            Arrays.fill(a, (byte) 0);\n        }\n    }\n\n    private static MessageDigest digest(String alg) {\n        try {\n            return MessageDigest.getInstance(alg);\n        } catch (NoSuchAlgorithmException impossible) {\n            throw new IllegalStateException(\"digest-missing:\" + alg, impossible);\n        }\n -&gt; password record. This is the only server-side state that\n * decides an authentication outcome.\n *\n * <h2>File format</h2>\n * <pre>\n * portal-roster v1\n * u=alice r=pbkdf2$sha256$150000$&lt;b64&gt;$&lt;b64&gt;\n * </pre>\n * One record per line, written by {@link #create}, {@link #rotateSecret}, {@link #setSecret}\n * or {@link #disableAccount} -- never by hand, and never by any path that does not go through\n * {@code mutateRoster}'s lock and atomic rewrite.\n * It contains no plaintext, no reversible transform and no hint; it is loaded\n * {@code 0600} inside a {@code 0700} directory and validated field by field.\n *\n * <h2>Loading is refuse-only</h2>\n * Every deviation is fatal to the load: an over-long line, an unknown header version, a\n * name outside {@link InputPolicy}, a record that does not parse, an iteration count\n * below {@link PasswordHasher#MIN_ITERATIONS}, a non-canonical base64 field, a duplicate\n * name, or more than {@link #MAX_ACCOUNTS} records. There is no \"skip the bad line and\n * carry on\" mode, because a roster that loads partially is a roster whose security\n * properties nobody can state.\n *\n * <p>The in-memory map is immutable for the lifetime of this object except through the\n * three mutators, each of which validates, persists atomically, and swaps the map under\n * one monitor.\n *\n * <h2>Cross-process writes</h2>\n * The roster is a whole-file read-modify-write, and the {@code portal} CLI is a short-lived\n * process: an operator enrolling eight accounts in a loop, or two admins on the same host,\n * runs several writers over one file. The monitor only serialises threads, so every mutator\n * additionally takes an exclusive {@link FileLock} on {@code employees.dat.lock} (the same\n * scheme as {@link Ledger}) and, while holding it, re-reads the file and applies its own\n * single-record change to what is on disk rather than to the map it loaded at start-up.\n * Without that reload the last writer wins and silently discards its siblings' accounts\n * while still reporting {@code created}. A name collision is therefore decided against the\n * disk too: exactly one of N concurrent {@code add-user} runs for the same name can win,\n * and the others report {@link CreateResult#NAME_TAKEN}.\n */\npublic final class EmployeeStore {\n\n    public static final String HEADER = \"portal-roster v1\";\n    public static final int MAX_ACCOUNTS = 50_000;\n    private static final int LINE_MAX_BYTES = 256;\n    private static final long MAX_FILE_BYTES = 8L * 1024 * 1024;\n\n    public enum CreateResult {\n        CREATED, NAME_TAKEN, WEAK_PASSWORD, INVALID_NAME, IO_FAILURE\n    }\n\n    private final Path file;\n    private final Path lockFile;\n    private final PasswordHasher hasher;\n    private final AuditLog audit;\n    private final Object monitor = new Object();\n    private volatile Map<String, String> records = Map.of();\n    /** Identity of the file the in-memory map was read from; see {@link #refreshIfChanged}. */\n    private volatile String fingerprint = \"<not-loaded>\";\n\n    public EmployeeStore(Path file, PasswordHasher hasher, AuditLog audit) {\n        this.file = file;\n        this.lockFile = file.resolveSibling(file.getFileName() + \".lock\");\n        this.hasher = hasher;\n        this.audit = audit;\n    }\n\n\n    /** Load an existing roster. Returns the number of accounts loaded. */\n    public int load() {\n        synchronized (monitor) {\n            if (!Files.exists(file, LinkOption.NOFOLLOW_LINKS)) {\n                records = Map.of();\n                fingerprint = fingerprintOf(file);\n                return 0;\n            }\n            String body = PrivateFiles.readPrivateBounded(file, MAX_FILE_BYTES);\n            records = parse(body);\n            fingerprint = fingerprintOf(file);\n            return records.size();\n        }\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Adopt a sibling process's roster change before the next authentication decision.\n     *\n     * <p>Without this, the long-lived {@code portal} process verifies against the copy it read\n     * at start-up while {@code portal disable-user} and {@code portal set-password} -- separate,\n     * short-lived processes -- edit the file underneath it. Measured: after {@code disable-user}\n     * had written {@code r=disabled$v1} for an account, the running portal went on answering\n     * {@code 303} for that account's old password, and after {@code set-password} replaced the\n     * credential the <em>superseded</em> password still produced {@code 303}. Both operations are\n     * the revocation path an operator believes they just used, so an account an administrator\n     * has disabled -- or whose password was rotated after a suspected leak -- keeps signing in\n     * until the service is restarted. Rule 3 says a failed credential check must fail closed;\n     * checking a stale record is not checking server-side state.\n     *\n     * <p>The check is one {@code stat}, and it is deliberately not about a name, so its cost is\n     * identical whether or not the presented account exists. Change is detected from the file's\n     * identity: every sanctioned write goes through {@code ATOMIC_MOVE}, which installs a\n     * <em>new inode</em>, so the file key alone distinguishes every write; size and mtime are\n     * included to also catch a hand edit that reused the inode. A roster that no longer parses\n     * is not treated as empty-and-fine and is not overwritten: the map becomes empty, so every\n     * account is refused as an unknown name, and the failure is logged. Refusing everybody is\n     * the correct direction; silently writing a roster reconstructed from a partial view would\n     * destroy accounts.\n     */\n    public void refreshIfChanged() {\n        String now = fingerprintOf(file);\n        if (now.equals(fingerprint)) {\n            return;\n        }\n        synchronized (monitor) {\n            if (now.equals(fingerprint)) {\n                return;\n            }\n            fingerprint = now;\n            try {\n                Map<String, String> reloaded = currentRosterOnDisk();\n                for (Map.Entry<String, String> e : reloaded.entrySet()) {\n                    hasher.requireValidRecord(e.getValue());\n                }\n                records = Collections.unmodifiableMap(reloaded);\n            } catch (RuntimeException unusable) {\n                // Fail closed, and remember the identity we just rejected so a broken file is\n                // parsed once rather than on every login.\n                records = Map.of();\n                audit.systemFailure(\"roster-refresh\", \"-\", \"-\", unusable);\n            }\n        }\n    }\n\n    /** Inode + size + mtime of the roster, or {@code absent}. Validates the same policy as enrolment. */\n    public CreateResult setSecret(String username, char[] secret) {\n        if (!InputPolicy.isUsername(username)) {\n            return CreateResult.INVALID_NAME;\n        }\n        String weak = InputPolicy.strengthFailure(secret, username.toCharArray());\n        if (weak != null) {\n            return CreateResult.WEAK_PASSWORD;\n        }\n        final boolean[] wasDisabled = new boolean[1];\n        final CreateResult[] verdict = new CreateResult[1];\n        synchronized (monitor) {\n            String record = hasher.hashWithCopy(secret, username.toCharArray());\n            mutateRoster((Map<String, String> onDisk) -> {\n                if (!onDisk.containsKey(username)) {\n                    verdict[0] = CreateResult.IO_FAILURE;\n                    return null;\n                }\n                // A disabled account's record is the unusable marker, so writing a real hash\n                // over it re-enables the account. There is deliberately no enable-user command,\n                // because an operation that turns an account back on must not be something that\n                // looks like a password change -- so we perform the reset the operator asked for\n                // and record, loudly, that it also lifted the disable.\n                wasDisabled[0] = PasswordHasher.DISABLED_RECORD.equals(onDisk.get(username));\n                Map<String, String> next = new LinkedHashMap<>(onDisk);\n                next.put(username, record);\n                verdict[0] = CreateResult.CREATED;\n                return next;\n            });\n        }\n        if (verdict[0] == CreateResult.CREATED) {\n            audit.event(\"set-secret\", Crypto.idRef(username), \"-\", \"ok\",\n                    wasDisabled[0] ? \"updated-re-enabled\" : \"updated\");\n        }\n        return verdict[0];\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Whether the account is administratively disabled. Exposed so the command line can tell\n     * an operator that a password reset is also lifting a disable, rather than leaving them\n     * to believe a resettable account was a live one.\n     */\n    public boolean isDisabled(String username) {\n        synchronized (monitor) {\n            return PasswordHasher.DISABLED_RECORD.equals(records.get(username));\n        }\n    }\n\n    /** Lock an account administratively by replacing its record with an unusable one. */\n    public void disableAccount(String username) {\n        final boolean[] applied = new boolean[1];\n        synchronized (monitor) {\n            mutateRoster((Map<String, String> onDisk) -> {\n                if (!onDisk.containsKey(username)) {\n                    return null;\n                }\n                Map<String, String> next = new LinkedHashMap<>(onDisk);\n                // The disabled marker is a recognised record shape that can never verify:\n                // PasswordHasher treats it as unparseable, runs the decoy KDF and returns\n                // false. It is deliberately NOT an invalid record, because an invalid record\n                // would fail the strict load and take the whole roster down with it.\n                next.put(username, PasswordHasher.DISABLED_RECORD);\n                applied[0] = true;\n                return next;\n            });\n        }\n        if (!applied[0]) {\n            throw new IllegalStateException(\"roster-name-unknown\");\n        }\n        audit.event(\"disable\", Crypto.idRef(username), \"-\", \"ok\", \"administrative\");\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Applies one record's change to the roster as it exists on disk, under an exclusive\n     * cross-process lock, and writes the result atomically. A {@code null} result from\n     * {@code edit} means \"decide against the disk and do nothing\"; either way the in-memory\n     * map is replaced by what the disk now holds, so a process never keeps acting on a view\n     * that a sibling writer has already invalidated.\n     */\n    private void mutateRoster(RosterEdit edit) {\n        FileChannel lockChannel = null;\n        try {\n            lockChannel = FileChannel.open(lockFile,\n                    java.util.EnumSet.of(java.nio.file.StandardOpenOption.CREATE,\n                            java.nio.file.StandardOpenOption.READ,\n                            java.nio.file.StandardOpenOption.WRITE),\n                    PosixFilePermissions.asFileAttribute(PrivateFiles.FILE_MODE));\n            FileLock lock = acquireLock(lockChannel);\n            try {\n                Map<String, String> onDisk = currentRosterOnDisk();\n                Map<String, String> next = edit.apply(onDisk);\n                if (next != null) {\n                    persistLocked(next);\n                    records = Collections.unmodifiableMap(next);\n                } else {\n                    records = Collections.unmodifiableMap(onDisk);\n                }\n                // Record the identity of whatever is now on disk (our own write installed a\n                // new inode; the refusal path left the file alone). Without this, every\n                // process would see a \"change\" on its next login and re-read every time.\n                fingerprint = fingerprintOf(file);\n            } finally {\n                try {\n                    lock.release();\n                } catch (IOException ignored) {\n                    // Closing the channel releases it.\n                }\n            }\n        } catch (IOException failure) {\n            throw new IllegalStateException(\"roster-io\", failure);\n        } finally {\n            if (lockChannel != null) {\n                try {\n                    lockChannel.close();\n                } catch (IOException ignored) {\n                    // Nothing further to do.\n                }\n            }\n        }\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Re-read and re-validate the roster from disk. An unparsable file is fatal rather than\n     * treated as empty: substituting an empty roster would make the next write erase every\n     * account, so a corrupt file must stop the mutator instead of being silently replaced.\n     */\n    private Map<String, String> currentRosterOnDisk() {\n        if (!Files.exists(file, LinkOption.NOFOLLOW_LINKS)) {\n            return Map.of();\n        }\n        return parse(PrivateFiles.readPrivateBounded(file, MAX_FILE_BYTES));\n    }\n\n    private FileLock acquireLock(FileChannel channel) throws IOException {\n        for (int attempt = 0; attempt < 400; attempt++) {\n            try {\n                FileLock lock = channel.tryLock(0L, Long.MAX_VALUE, false);\n                if (lock != null) {\n                    return lock;\n                }\n            } catch (OverlappingFileLockException heldByThisJvm) {\n                // Another thread of this JVM holds it; the monitor above already serialises\n                // this path, so this can only be a momentary race with a sibling store.\n            } catch (java.nio.channels.ClosedByInterruptException gone) {\n                throw gone;\n            } catch (IOException contended) {\n                // Fall through to the next attempt.\n            }\n            try {\n                Thread.sleep(25L);\n            } catch (InterruptedException interrupted) {\n                Thread.currentThread().interrupt();\n                throw new IOException(\"roster-interrupted\", interrupted);\n            }\n        }\n        throw new IOException(\"roster-lock-contended\");\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * The KDF runs before the lock is taken so that concurrent enrolments do not queue\n     * behind each other's digests; the record is applied to whatever is on disk once the\n     * lock is held.\n     */\n    private interface RosterEdit {\n        /** @return the roster to persist, or {@code null} to leave the file untouched. */\n        Map<String, String> apply(Map<String, String> onDisk);\n    }\n\n    private void persistLocked(Map<String, String> next) {\n        StringBuilder sb = new StringBuilder(next.size() * 96 + 64);\n        sb.append(HEADER).append('\\n');\n        for (Map.Entry<String, String> e : next.entrySet()) {\n            sb.append(\"u=\").append(e.getKey()).append(\" r=\").append(e.getValue()).append('\\n');\n        }\n        PrivateFiles.writePrivateAtomic(file, sb.toString().getBytes(StandardCharsets.US_ASCII));\n    }\n\n    /** Write a fresh roster (fails if the file already exists). */\n    public void initialiseEmpty() {\n        synchronized (monitor) {\n            PrivateFiles.createPrivateNew(file, (HEADER + \"\\n\").getBytes(StandardCharsets.US_ASCII));\n            records = Map.of();\n            fingerprint = fingerprintOf(file);\n        }\n    }\n\n    public Path path() {\n        return file;\n    }\n}\n"}]}},{"type":"user","uuid":"4a341f6c-cae3-4161-9975-10479ac30c2c","session_id":"43a3d1fe-7aa6-43e7-b332-fdd94a07da51","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"tool_result","tool_use_id":"call_9ec89da43a074239839f36e4","is_error":false,"content":"package com.portal.auth;\n\nimport java.time.Duration;\nimport java.util.ArrayList;\nimport java.util.Iterator;\nimport java.util.LinkedHashMap;\nimport java.util.List;\nimport java.util.Map;\nimport java.util.Optional;\n\n/**\n * Server-side session and CSRF-token state.\n *\n * <p>A session id is <b>generated here</b> from {@link SecureRandom} (256 bits); it is\n * never accepted from a caller as proof of anything. What the cookie carries is only a\n * lookup key: {@link #find} validates the wire shape, hashes the value, and requires a\n * live server-side entry that has not expired. No entry, expired entry, wrong shape\n * =&gt; anonymous. A forged or guessed cookie therefore grants exactly nothing, and a\n * dumped map does not yield usable ids because only SHA-256(id) is stored.\n *\n * <p>Both maps are bounded and evict oldest-first, so a client that mints requests\n * cannot grow the heap without limit.\n *\n * <h2>Remaining risk, stated plainly</h2>\n * A session id stolen from a browser (XSS, malware, a shared workstation) is usable by\n * the thief until it expires. Mitigations: {@code HttpOnly} (no script reads it),\n * {@code Secure} + HTTPS-only listener (no network sniffing), {@code SameSite=Strict}\n * (not sent on cross-site requests), 20-minute idle / 8-hour absolute lifetime, and a\n * logout that deletes the server-side entry. Full defence needs per-device key\n * confirmation, which is outside this feature.\n */\npublic final class SessionManager {\n\n    public static final Duration IDLE_TIMEOUT = Duration.ofMinutes(20);\n    public static final Duration ABSOLUTE_TIMEOUT = Duration.ofHours(8);\n    public static final Duration CSRF_TIMEOUT = Duration.ofMinutes(15);\n    public static final int MAX_SESSIONS = 10_000;\n    public static final int MAX_PENDING_CSRF = 50_000;\n\n    public static final class Session {\n        public final String username;\n        final long issuedAtEpochSecond;\n        long lastUsedEpochSecond;\n\n        Session(String username, long now) {\n            this.username = username;\n            this.issuedAtEpochSecond = now;\n            this.lastUsedEpochSecond = now;\n        }\n    }\n\n    private final Object monitor = new Object();\n\n    /** Bounded, insertion-ordered so eviction is deterministic.
